```text
     1	\section{Introduction}
     2	\label{sec:intro}
     3	
     4	The text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.
     5	As synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.
     6	This model$\to$data$\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical; it is already occurring in the wild. Yet whether the recursive generation depth of a text can be estimated from its statistical properties remains unstudied, a question that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.
     7	In this work, we focus on the 1--1.5B parameter regime under \emph{controlled full-replacement chains}, where each generation fully replaces the prior training set, isolating the depth variable. Real-world contamination typically involves partial mixing (accumulation), where synthetic and human data co-exist at unknown proportions; the full-replacement setting provides an upper bound on depth signal strength, and we discuss implications for accumulation settings in \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
     8	
     9	Existing tools address adjacent but distinct problems.
    10	Binary detectors \cite{binoculars2024,fast_detectgpt2024,diveye2026} collapse all depths $d \geq 1$ into a single category, a limitation we confirm empirically: their pairwise d1-vs-d2 AUC collapses to chance level (\S\ref{sec:gen:baselines}); watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding.
    11	The model collapse literature \cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.
    12	No existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone (Figure~\ref{fig:problem}).
    13	
    14	We find that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are \textbf{consistent in direction but scale-dependent in magnitude} across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.
    15	Specifically, the perplexity tail feature family (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{p75\_ppl}, \texttt{kurtosis\_ppl}) and surprisal variance emerge as dominant depth indicators, with group-level dominance robust across all 9 model--strategy configurations and three importance measures.
    16	Following the tradition of benchmark-driven empirical studies in NLP, our contribution centers on task formalization and systematic empirical characterization rather than algorithmic novelty; the convergence of four classifiers within 0.8\,pp (\S\ref{sec:gen:baselines}) confirms that discriminative signal resides in feature design, not classifier architecture.
    17	We formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and find that a lightweight pipeline suffices: compute 19 reference-model statistical features (effective dimensionality ${\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \S\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.
    18	To characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \times 3$ grid of model families $\times$ decoding strategies spanning 4 depths (180K cell-instances).
    19	The atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories and we introduce \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD), to our knowledge the first operational metric for ordinal depth resolution in recursive generation, to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.
    20	
    21	\begin{figure*}[t]
    22	\centering
    23	\includegraphics[width=0.68\textwidth]{figures/fig_problem_illustration.pdf}
    24	\caption{Recursive generation depth estimation. A seed corpus $\mathcal{D}_0$ feeds a LoRA finetuning--sampling loop producing successively deeper corpora. Given a passage~$x$, the task is to estimate its ordinal depth~$d$.}
    25	\label{fig:problem}
    26	\end{figure*}
    27	Our contributions are:
    28	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
    29	    \item \textbf{Task formalization and metric.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, introduce the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, and construct a 180K cell-instance benchmark ($3$ model families $\times$ $3$ decoding strategies $\times$ $4$ depths; 140K unique passages).
    30	
    31	    \item \textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} The perplexity tail feature family and surprisal variance jointly dominate depth discrimination across all 9 cells; $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ in 8/9 cells (4 under nucleus depth extension), validated by 5-class extension (71--80\%); feature group ablation confirms perplexity tail dominance (76.6\% alone vs.\ 78.4\% full set).
    32	
    33	    \item \textbf{Systematic characterization.} Cross-model transfer 66.4\% (d1--d3: 55.4\%), cross-domain arXiv 74.1\%, 14B cross-reference 44.1\% ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$); 25--50\% accumulation preserves $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ in 5/6 cells despite $-$8--15\,pp accuracy attenuation. We identify cross-strategy transfer (d1--d3: 45.9\%) as a boundary condition.
    34	\end{itemize}
```

     1	\section{Generalization and Robustness}
     2	\label{sec:generalization}
     3	
     4	We test whether atlas accuracy (68.9--85.9\% four-class; d1--d3: 58.9--81.5\%) reflects genuine statistical regularities across eight generalization axes (five below, three in Appendix).
     5	
     6	\subsection{Classifier Comparison and Surface-Text Ablation}
     7	\label{sec:gen:baselines}
     8	
     9	Table~\ref{tab:baselines} compares methods across all 9 cells.
    10	Four classifiers (RF, XGBoost, ordinal MLP, prototypical) converge within 0.8\,pp under cross-model transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:learned_estimator}), confirming a feature-determined accuracy ceiling.
    11	
    12	\paragraph{Neural end-to-end baselines.}
    13	DeBERTa-v3-base (LoRA) underperforms RF by 16--32\,pp (d2 recall 8.4\%); RoBERTa-DCOL (ordinal contrastive; 64.5\%) narrows the gap but remains 14\,pp below RF.
    14	
    15	\paragraph{Binary detectors and membership inference.}
    16	Perturbation-based (DetectGPT, 28.8\%) and membership-inference (Min-K\% Prob, 33.2\%; Min-K\%++ \cite{zhang2024_minkpp} comparable) methods produce scores with no ordinal resolution (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}); pairwise analysis confirms ordinal collapse: Binoculars d1v2 AUC\,=\,0.50--0.53 vs.\ RF 0.93 (Appendix~\ref{app:detector_baselines}).
    17	CORAL calibration does not close the gap (d1--d3: 40.4\% vs.\ RF 71.6\%).
    18	Binoculars \cite{binoculars2024}, calibrated with 11-feature RF, independently converges to 78.1\% ($p{>}0.05$ vs.\ our 78.4\%); merging all 30 features yields 82.6\% (Appendix~\ref{app:feature_ablation_bino}).
    19	
    20	\paragraph{Surface-text ablation.}
    21	Single-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\%); zero-shot LLMs (best 36.2\%; Appendix~\ref{app:zeroshot_prompt}) also fail; depth signal resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text.
    22	
    23	\begin{table}[t]
    24	\centering
    25	\small
    26	\caption{Method comparison (RF mean across 9 cells, 5-fold CV). Chance = 25\% / 33.3\% (d1--d3). $^\dagger$Adapted from binary detection; Binoculars 11-feat: $p{>}0.05$ vs.\ 19-feat RF (paired permutation test).}
    27	\label{tab:baselines}
    28	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
    29	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}
    30	\toprule
    31	Method & Calibration & 4-Cl (\%) & d1--d3 (\%) \\
    32	\midrule
    33	RF 19-feature (ours) & RF & 78.4 & \textbf{71.6} \\
    34	Binoculars$^\dagger$ \cite{binoculars2024} & RF (11 feat) & 78.1 & --- \\
    35	Merged (30 feat)$^\dagger$ & RF & \textbf{82.6} & --- \\
    36	Binoculars \cite{binoculars2024} & threshold & 69.8 & --- \\
    37	Binoculars \cite{binoculars2024} & RF (1 feat) & 63.7 & --- \\
    38	RoBERTa-DCOL (ordinal) & contrastive & 64.5 & --- \\
    39	DeBERTa-v3 (end-to-end) & cross-entropy & 53.1--60.6 & --- \\
    40	\midrule
    41	Zero-shot LLM (best) & --- & 36.2 & --- \\
    42	Min-K\% Prob \cite{shi2024_minkprob} & CORAL & 33.2 & 40.4 \\
    43	DetectGPT \cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt} & best-of-3 & 28.8 & 24.3 \\
    44	Random & --- & 25.0 & 33.3 \\
    45	\bottomrule
    46	\end{tabular}}
    47	\end{table}
    48	
    49	\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}
    50	\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}
    51	
    52	\begin{table}[t]
    53	\centering
    54	\small
    55	\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Chance = 25\% / 33\% (d1--d3). d1--d3 mean 55.4\%; QWK\,=\,0.81; 94.8\% errors at $|\Delta| \leq 1$.}
    56	\label{tab:cross_model}
    57	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
    58	\toprule
    59	Holdout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\
    60	 & (\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\
    61	\midrule
    62	Pythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\
    63	OLMo & \textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\
    64	GPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\
    65	\midrule
    66	Mean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\
    67	\bottomrule
    68	\end{tabular}
    69	\end{table}
    70	
    71	Mean transfer accuracy is 66.4\% (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}; d1--d3: 55.4\%, $2.7{\times}$ chance); d2 worst case 26.8\% (Pythia holdout; confusion matrices in Figure~\ref{fig:confusion_matrix}).
    72	
    73	\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}
    74	\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}
    75	
    76	Cross-strategy transfer reveals a structural barrier: d1--d3 balanced accuracy drops to 45.9\% (four-class: 59.1\%), 7\,pp below cross-model transfer (Table~\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).
    77	A two-stage pipeline (strategy classifier $\to$ conditioned depth classifier) recovers much of this gap: 70.5\% four-class accuracy ($+$11.5\,pp over LOO); oracle routing yields 78.3\%, so the 7.8\,pp residual is attributable to strategy misidentification (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_discussion}).
    78	Our depth estimation claims hold most strongly within-strategy or when the decoding configuration is known.
    79	
    80	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
    81	\subsection{Preliminary Cross-Scale Observations (14B)}
    82	\label{sec:gen:crossscale}
    83	
    84	At 14B (Qwen2.5-14B), self-reference yields 50.8\% ($2{\times}$ chance, $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$); cross-reference control (Pythia-1.4B) drops accuracy to 44.1\% ($-$6.8\,pp) but raises $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ to 2 for nucleus and temperature, revealing that ${\sim}$7\,pp of the accuracy drop is attributable to reference-model mismatch rather than scale (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).
    85	Cross-domain (arXiv) accuracy reaches 74.1\% at 1--1.5B; cross-domain FPR is discussed in Limitations.

```text
     1	\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}
     2	\label{sec:atlas}
     3	
     4	We present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.
     5	The atlas spans $3 \times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells at the 1--1.5B parameter scale, evaluated at depths $d \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
     6	
     7	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
     8	\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}
     9	\label{sec:atlas:id}
    10	
    11	Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.
    12	Accuracy ranges from 68.9\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.9\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature) four-class (d1--d3 balanced: 58.9--81.5\%), all exceeding the 25\%/33\% chance baselines.
    13	Two axis effects emerge.
    14	GPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (75.8--85.9\%), followed by OLMo (74.4--82.3\%) and Pythia (68.9--78.7\%).
    15	Top-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.
    16	The mean within-model strategy gap is 9.3~pp, comparable to the 7.2~pp mean within-strategy model gap; decoding strategy contributes at least as much as model family to performance variance.
    17	
    18	\begin{table}[t]
    19	\centering
    20	\small
    21	\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\%) with bootstrap 95\% CI (${\pm}$half-width; $B{=}10{,}000$). RF with 200 trees, evaluated via 5-fold stratified CV (seed=42). Parenthesized: d1--d3 balanced accuracy from the same 4-class RF (chance = 25\% / 33\%). Depth-0 recall exceeds 97\% in all cells. Within-distribution ordinal quality: mean absolute error (MAE)\,=\,0.23, QWK\,=\,0.89 (QWK$_{\text{d1--d3}}$\,=\,0.74); 98.2\% of errors fall on adjacent depths ($|\Delta| \leq 1$). $^\dagger$Self-reference scoring (Pythia-1.4B is both generator and reference model); 4--11\,pp advantage over cross-model references (Appendix~\ref{app:ref_ablation}). Cross-cell comparisons involving Pythia should be interpreted with this caveat.}
    22	\label{tab:main_matrix}
    23	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
    24	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
    25	\toprule
    26	& Nucleus & Temp.\ $\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\
    27	\midrule
    28	Pythia-1.4B$^\dagger$  & 78.7{\scriptsize$\pm$0.5}\,(71.8) & 76.8{\scriptsize$\pm$0.5}\,(69.3) & 68.9{\scriptsize$\pm$0.6}\,(58.9) \\
    29	OLMo-1B     & 78.9{\scriptsize$\pm$0.5}\,(72.7) & 82.3{\scriptsize$\pm$0.5}\,(76.6) & 74.4{\scriptsize$\pm$0.5}\,(66.2) \\
    30	GPT-2~XL    & \textbf{84.3}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.5}\,(79.6) & \textbf{85.9}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.5}\,(81.5) & \textbf{75.8}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.6}\,(68.1) \\
    31	\bottomrule
    32	\end{tabular}}
    33	\end{table}
    34	
    35	\paragraph{Per-class recall.}
    36	Depth-0 recall exceeds 97\% in all 9 cells.
    37	Among generated depths, d2 consistently yields the lowest recall (44--73\%), reflecting its position at the center of the depth gradient where features overlap with both d1 and d3, consistent with d2-vs-d3 as the EDD bottleneck (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}; full per-class recall in Appendix~\ref{app:per_class}).
    38	
    39	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
    40	\subsection{Feature Consistency Across the Grid}
    41	\label{sec:atlas:features}
    42	
    43	Multicollinearity is substantial (VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 of 19 features); importance rankings below indicate feature \emph{family} contributions rather than individual effects; permutation importance (Appendix~\ref{app:permutation}) partially mitigates collinearity bias \cite{strobl2008_conditional,hooker2021_permutation}.
    44	Despite the 17.1~pp accuracy spread across cells, the perplexity tail statistics family (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{p75\_ppl}, \texttt{kurtosis\_ppl}) and surprisal variance (\texttt{var\_surprisal}) dominate depth discrimination in every setting.
    45	Within this family, \texttt{p90\_ppl} and \texttt{var\_surprisal} are consistently prominent, though individual rankings should be interpreted cautiously given VIF${>}$10; group-level ablation (\S\ref{sec:atlas:features}, below) provides the collinearity-robust confirmation.\footnote{The group-level dominance of perplexity tail statistics is robust across importance measures (Gini, SHAP, permutation; Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$).}
    46	Conditional permutation importance \citep{strobl2008_conditional} across all nine cells confirms \texttt{var\_surprisal} and \texttt{p90\_ppl} as the top-2 features in 8/9 cells (average bootstrap rank 1.4 and 1.7; CI width $\leq 0.5$), consistent with Gini-based rankings despite VIF $> 10$ (Appendix~\ref{app:conditional_pi}).
    47	Table~\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking; Figure~\ref{fig:atlas_heatmap} visualizes the importance landscape across all nine cells.
    48	
    49	Cross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\bar{\rho} = 0.868$ ($[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information despite differing in absolute accuracy.
    50	Top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\rho = 0.811$), as vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape at the cost of weaker signal (\S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).
    51	
    52	Feature ablation confirms this concentration: the top-5 features achieve 97.6\% of full accuracy (76.5\% vs.\ 78.4\%); group-level ablation shows perplexity features alone reach 76.6\%, surprisal 73.2\%, lexical diversity ${\leq}$47\%, and repetition ${\leq}$40\%.\footnote{Best single feature (\texttt{p90\_ppl}) achieves 58.3\%, providing a more competitive single-feature baseline than mean perplexity (25.1\%).}
    53	
    54	The feature hierarchy shifts with depth.
    55	At shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\ d2), \texttt{var\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\ d4, d4 vs.\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{kurtosis\_ppl}) take over.
    56	This transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).
    57	
    58	\begin{table}[t]
    59	\centering
    60	\small
    61	\caption{Top-5 features by Gini importance. \textbf{Bold}: top-2. \texttt{p90\_ppl} top-2 in 9/9; \texttt{var\_surprisal} 8/9 under SHAP (7/9 under Gini). Individual rankings are approximate due to multicollinearity (VIF${>}$10 for 14--16/19 features).}
    62	\label{tab:feature_importance}
    63	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
    64	\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
    65	\toprule
    66	Cell & Top-5 features (Gini importance, ranked) \\
    67	\midrule
    68	Pythia, nuc.       & \textbf{var\_surprisal\,(.17)}, \textbf{p90\_ppl\,(.15)}, mean\_ppl\,(.11), var\_ppl\,(.08), p75\_ppl\,(.07) \\
    69	Pythia, $\tau$     & \textbf{var\_surprisal\,(.17)}, \textbf{p90\_ppl\,(.14)}, mean\_ppl\,(.11), p75\_ppl\,(.07), mean\_surprisal\,(.07) \\
    70	Pythia, top-$k$    & \textbf{var\_surprisal\,(.16)}, \textbf{p90\_ppl\,(.12)}, mean\_ppl\,(.11), var\_ppl\,(.07), mean\_surprisal\,(.06) \\
    71	GPT-2\,XL, nuc.    & \textbf{var\_surprisal\,(.21)}, \textbf{p90\_ppl\,(.21)}, p75\_ppl\,(.10), mean\_surprisal\,(.07), mean\_ppl\,(.06) \\
    72	GPT-2\,XL, $\tau$  & \textbf{p90\_ppl\,(.25)}, \textbf{p75\_ppl\,(.15)}, mean\_surprisal\,(.13), var\_surprisal\,(.12), p50\_ppl\,(.05) \\
    73	GPT-2\,XL, top-$k$ & \textbf{p90\_ppl\,(.24)}, \textbf{p75\_ppl\,(.12)}, var\_surprisal\,(.10), mean\_surprisal\,(.09), type\_token\_ratio\,(.04) \\
    74	OLMo, nuc.         & \textbf{p90\_ppl\,(.18)}, \textbf{var\_surprisal\,(.15)}, p75\_ppl\,(.11), mean\_surprisal\,(.07), type\_token\_ratio\,(.06) \\
    75	OLMo, $\tau$       & \textbf{p90\_ppl\,(.21)}, \textbf{var\_surprisal\,(.16)}, p75\_ppl\,(.12), mean\_surprisal\,(.09), type\_token\_ratio\,(.05) \\
    76	OLMo, top-$k$      & \textbf{p90\_ppl\,(.20)}, \textbf{var\_surprisal\,(.17)}, p75\_ppl\,(.10), mean\_surprisal\,(.07), mean\_ppl\,(.05) \\
    77	\bottomrule
    78	\end{tabular}}
    79	\end{table}
    80	
    81	\begin{figure}[t]
    82	\centering
    83	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_atlas_heatmap.pdf}
    84	\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap for the top-7 features. Rows: features ranked by mean Gini importance; columns: 9 model--strategy cells. Stars mark the two universal top-2 features (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{var\_surprisal}). Full 19-feature breakdown in Appendix~\ref{app:feature_ablation}.}
    85	\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}
    86	\end{figure}
    87	
    88	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
    89	\subsection{EDD Analysis}
    90	\label{sec:atlas:edd}
    91	\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}
    92	
    93	Depth features exhibit strong monotonic trends (Spearman $\rho = -1.0$ for perplexity tail statistics; Figure~\ref{fig:feature_trajectory}); inter-depth distances are non-uniform (d0-vs-d1 AUC ${>}$0.99 in all 9 cells, d2-vs-d3: 0.69--0.89).
    94	
    95	We extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.
    96	Each model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (Pythia-1.4B).
    97	We train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\ref{eq:edd}.
    98	
    99	\paragraph{AUC decay and cross-model convergence.}
   100	For Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically from $0.999$ (d0v1) to $0.683$ (d4v5); AUC-excess contraction ratios (0.80 at d1v2$\to$d2v3, 0.69 at d3v4$\to$d4v5) show convergence toward indistinguishability accelerates with depth (Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay}).
   101	The d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near $T{=}0.75$ for all three models (Pythia $0.784$, OLMo $0.765$, GPT-2~XL $0.750$), while d4-vs-d5 converges to a narrow band ($0.681$--$0.684$, range $0.003$), suggesting an architecture-independent depth ceiling within the 1--1.5B nucleus setting.
   102	Bootstrap testing ($H_0{:}\, \mathrm{AUC} \leq 0.75$; Bonferroni-corrected for 3 comparisons, $\alpha{=}0.0167$) yields $p = 0.001$ (Pythia), $0.091$ (OLMo), $0.505$ (GPT-2~XL).
   103	
   104	\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}
   105	Across the 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3), all cells achieve $\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ at $T{=}0.65$; at $T{=}0.70$, 8/9 cells; at $T{=}0.75$, 7/9; at $T{=}0.80$, 5/9.
   106	Top-$k$ configurations lose $\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$); d2-vs-d3 is the consistent bottleneck.
   107	
   108	\begin{figure}[t]
   109	\centering
   110	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_auc_decay.pdf}
   111	\caption{AUC decay curves for Pythia-1.4B (nucleus, depths 0--5) under three pipeline configurations: binary pairwise RF, 4-class RF, and 6-class RF. Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$. All curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\% CIs.}
   112	\label{fig:auc_decay}
   113	\end{figure}
   114	
   115	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
   116	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}
   117	\label{sec:atlas:strategy}
   118	
   119	The three strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories.
   120	Temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$) yields the highest accuracy in 2/3 models (GPT-2~XL 85.9\%, OLMo 82.3\%) because uniform logit scaling accelerates convergence to an attractor~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, amplifying the fingerprint but creating a boundary reversal risk under cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:generalization}).
   121	Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$) produces the lowest accuracy (68.9--75.8\%) because hard vocabulary truncation removes depth-discriminative low-probability tokens.
   122	The strategy effect (9.3\,pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.2\,pp); deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy matters at least as much as the generator model.
```

```text
     1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
     2	\label{sec:discussion}
     3	
     4	\paragraph{Multi-channel depth encoding.}
     5	Three importance measures (Gini, SHAP, permutation; Kendall's $W{=}0.90$), confirmed by a fourth (CPI; Appendix~\ref{app:conditional_pi}), converge on the same feature ranking, and an independent Binoculars-derived 11-feature pathway reaches statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1\% vs.\ 78.4\%, $p{>}0.05$; merged: 82.6\%).
     6	Recursive generation thus leaves fingerprints across multiple statistical channels; the depth signal is a distributional property, not a single exploitable artifact.
     7	
     8	\paragraph{Deployment considerations.}
     9	The strategy effect (9.3\,pp) exceeding the model effect (7.2\,pp) means auditing pipelines must condition on decoding configuration, motivating the two-stage pipeline (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) and echoing the call by \citet{schaeffer2025} for operationally precise metrics.
    10	A preliminary curation PoC (Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}) is inconclusive: RF-filtered subsets outperform random subsampling but do not significantly improve over unfiltered data ($p{>}0.4$).
    11	Scale attenuation at 14B (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), consistent with tail-narrowing theory \citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, may be partially recoverable via scale-matched references.
    12	%% --- 7B SCALE TEMPLATES (swap in when results arrive) ---
    13	%% VERSION A (7B ≥60%, EDD≥2): replace the sentence above with:
    14	%% Scale attenuation is monotonic across the three scales tested: self-reference accuracy declines from 78.7\% (1.4B) through $X$\% (7B, $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}Y$) to 50.8\% (14B, $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$), consistent with tail-narrowing theory \citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; the within-family Qwen 7B$\to$14B comparison ($-Z$\,pp) isolates a scale effect free of architecture confounds, and whether scale-matched references can recover further signal remains open.
    15	%% VERSION B (7B 40-60%): replace with:
    16	%% Scale attenuation is confirmed at an intermediate scale: 7B self-reference accuracy reaches $X$\% ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}Y$), between 1.4B (78.7\%) and 14B (50.8\%), consistent with monotonic attenuation \citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} though insufficient to characterize its functional form; whether scale-matched references can recover further signal remains open.
    17	%% --- END TEMPLATES ---
```

```text
     1	\section*{Limitations}
     2	\label{sec:limitations}
     3	
     4	\paragraph{Full-replacement vs.\ accumulation.}
     5	The primary benchmark uses full-replacement chains; real-world contamination involves accumulation at unknown mixing ratios \cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate}.
     6	At 25--50\% synthetic mixing, accuracy drops 8--15\,pp and $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ is preserved in 5/6 tested cells (GPT-2~XL nucleus at 50\% drops to $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$; d2v3 AUC\,=\,0.695, marginally below threshold; Table~\ref{tab:accum_auc}); accumulation experiments use matched strategies per model (nucleus for GPT-2~XL/Pythia, temperature for OLMo) and do not test cross-strategy accumulation; extension to larger models and higher mixing ratios remains future work.
     7	
     8	\paragraph{Scale, generalization, and reference model.}
     9	At 14B, cross-reference accuracy drops to 44.1\% ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$), with reference-model mismatch accounting for ${\sim}$7\,pp; cross-strategy transfer (d1--d3: 45.9\%) can invert d1/d2 predictions (d1v2 AUC as low as 0.13 for temperature text, 0.25 for OLMo, classified by a nucleus-trained model), requiring strategy-aware deployment.
    10	The pipeline requires a fixed reference model; cross-architecture references degrade accuracy by 4--11\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:ref_ablation}).
    11	Additional caveats (single LoRA seed with ${\sim}$1--3\,pp uncertainty, web-crawl false positive rates, passage length sensitivity) are detailed in the Appendix.
    12	
    13	\section*{Ethics Statement}
    14	
    15	This work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.
    16	No human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.
    17	Our method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.
    18	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
    19	AI assistants were used in an auxiliary role for coding support and language polishing.
    20	All scientific decisions, experimental design, analysis, and core writing were conducted by the authors.
```

```text
     1	\section{Related Work}
     2	\label{sec:related}
     3	
     4	\paragraph{Model Collapse Theory.}
     5	Recursive training on model outputs erodes distributional tails \cite{shumailov2024_nature}, an effect formalized as exponential tail decay via scaling laws \cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails,seddik2024synthetic} and extended to autophagous loops in the MAD framework \cite{alemohammad2024_mad} and Markovian generation chains \cite{geng2026_markovian}.
     6	Subsequent work refines the conditions under which collapse occurs: \citet{bertrand2024_stability} identify convergence to a fixed point under mixed real-synthetic retraining (providing theoretical grounding for our EDD ceiling, \S\ref{sec:method:theory}), \citet{shi2025_closer_look} reframe collapse as a generalization-to-memorization transition, and \citet{dohmatob2025_strong_collapse} extend guarantees to broader model classes.
     7	Mitigation \cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate,drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse,yi2026_escaping_collapse,falahati2026_curated,wenzek2020_ccnet}, data scaling under constraint \cite{muennighoff2023scaling}, non-monotonic dynamics \cite{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement}, convergence analysis \cite{marchi2024_heat_death,wang2026_contamination,knowledge_collapse2025,dohmatob2024_demystified}, and diversity decline \cite{guo2024curious,briesch2024selfconsuming,padmakumar2024diversity,sigma2026_spectral,synthetic_eggs2026} are active areas.
     8	\citet{kazdan2025_collapse_or_thrive} systematically compare training workflows and show that data accumulation avoids collapse while pure replacement does not; \citet{suresh2025_rate_collapse} characterize the rate of collapse for fundamental distributions under recursive maximum-likelihood estimation.
     9	\citet{schaeffer2025} argue that ``model collapse'' conflates multiple phenomena and call for operationally precise metrics; we respond by operationalizing tail-erosion predictions into a deployable ordinal classifier that reveals strategy-dependent trajectories and non-monotonic 7B behavior that the theory does not predict.
    10	All assume depth is known a priori; we address the inverse problem of estimating depth from text alone.
    11	
    12	\paragraph{AI Text Detection.}
    13	The detection literature \cite{wu2025_detection_survey} spans statistical methods (DetectGPT \cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt}, Fast-DetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars2024}, DivEye \cite{diveye2026}, BiScope \cite{guo2024_biscope}, GLTR \cite{gehrmann2019_gltr}, Ghostbuster \cite{verma2024_ghostbuster}, DNA-GPT \cite{yang2024_dnagpt}), geometric approaches based on intrinsic dimensionality \cite{tulchinskii2023_intrinsic_dim}, and watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark}, with known limits from paraphrasing \cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase}, distribution shift \cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and generator barriers \cite{pu2026_generator_barrier}.
    14	\citet{wang2023_seqxgpt} address sentence-level attribution by using log-probability features from white-box LLMs.
    15	M4-SemEval \cite{wang2024_m4_semeval}, MAGE \cite{li2024_mage}, and the PAN 2026 shared tasks \cite{bevendorff2026_pan} benchmark detection across domains, models, and adversarial conditions; RAID \cite{dugan2024_raid} targets binary detection across diverse attack vectors rather than ordinal depth estimation, making direct protocol adoption inapplicable to our task.
    16	\citet{bevendorff2025_two_paradigms} distinguish authorship attribution (which model?) from verification (human or AI?); depth estimation introduces a third axis: which recursive iteration.
    17	All these methods perform binary classification, discarding ordinal depth information; our approach outputs ordinal depth labels, complementing rather than replacing binary detection.
    18	
    19	\paragraph{Membership Inference and Data Provenance.}
    20	\citet{carlini2021_extracting} demonstrate that training data can be extracted from LLMs, motivating provenance-aware pipelines.
    21	Min-K\% Prob \cite{shi2024_minkprob} detects pretraining membership via low-probability token statistics, related to our perplexity features but limited to binary classification; Datamodels \cite{ilyas2022datamodels} trace predictions to influential training examples; cryptographically undetectable watermarks \cite{christ2024_undetectable} require generator cooperation.
    22	TROVE \cite{zhu2025_trove} introduces fine-grained sentence-level provenance tracing with relationship classification, but targets source attribution rather than recursive depth.
    23	Depth estimation occupies a distinct niche: ordinal, retroactive, and cooperation-free.
    24	
    25	\paragraph{Adjacent Tasks.}
    26	EditLens \cite{editlens2026} estimates editing extent but does not model recursive chains; \citet{uchendu2020_attribution} classify \emph{which} model generated text; \citet{mohamed2025_broken_telephone} characterize \emph{how} text degrades under iterative generation without classifying which iteration a passage comes from.
    27	Our task performs the inverse: ordinal depth estimation from text alone.
    28	
    29	\paragraph{Ordinal Classification.}
    30	CORAL \cite{coral2020} enforces rank consistency via ordered binary tasks; CLOC \cite{cloc2025} extends contrastive learning with multi-margin ordinal losses; \citet{kasa2024_ordinality} benchmark ordinal techniques for NLP; \citet{janitza2016_ordinal_rf} show RF implicitly respects ordinal structure when inter-class distances are monotonic.
    31	We adopt CORAL and CLOC as baselines (\S\ref{sec:generalization}).
    32	
    33	\paragraph{Decoding Strategy Effects.}
    34	Decoding strategies shape text statistics in ways that interact with depth signals \cite{holtzman2020_nucleus,finlayson2024_closing,garces_arias2025_decoding_decoded,wiher2022_decoding,basu2021_mirostat}.
    35	Our atlas (\S\ref{sec:atlas}) shows these strategies induce qualitatively different degradation trajectories under recursive generation.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Qr1YLhzoQKhDr45e24mpbJ`</summary>

```text
     1	\subsection{Theoretical Motivation}
     2	\label{sec:method:theory}
     3	
     4	Recursive generation contracts the support of the learned distribution, with low-probability tokens lost first \cite{shumailov2024_nature}: tail mass $P_k(\mathrm{ppl} > t)$ decreases monotonically in depth $k$.
     5	This tail erosion predicts the perplexity tail feature family as a depth marker, confirmed jointly with surprisal variance across all 9 cells (\S\ref{sec:atlas:features}).
     6	Surprisal variance captures complementary distributional narrowing: as the support contracts, per-token surprise becomes more predictable.
     7	
     8	\paragraph{EDD upper bound.}
     9	Tail mass at depth $k$ scales as $\alpha^k$ for a contraction factor $\alpha \in (0,1)$ \cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; EDD reaches its upper bound when adjacent-depth feature distance falls below classifier resolution.
    10	Our empirical AUC-excess contraction (ratio 0.80 at d1v2$\to$d2v3, 0.69 at d3v4$\to$d4v5; \S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) is consistent with geometric decay approaching a fixed point \cite{bertrand2024_stability}.
    11	5-class extensions to d4 (71--80\%, chance = 20\%; Appendix~\ref{app:depth4_ext}) confirm depth~4 discriminability; mean perplexity saturates before tail statistics, placing a physical ceiling on EDD.
    12	
    13	\paragraph{Scale-dependent attenuation.}
    14	Larger models approximate the training distribution more faithfully per step ($\alpha$ closer to 1), predicting weaker per-step signal.
    15	Qwen2.5-14B supports this: d0-vs-d1 AUC drops to 0.643 (vs.\ ${>}0.99$ at 1--1.5B; \S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}); however, 7--8B results are mixed ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ ranging from 4 to 5), suggesting confounds (QLoRA quantization, architecture) interact with the scale effect (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).
    16	
    17	\paragraph{Strategy-dependent convergence.}
    18	The contraction rate $\alpha$ varies with decoding strategy.
    19	Temperature scaling ($\tau < 1$) sharpens the logit distribution uniformly and accelerates convergence to the mode; temperature depth-$d$ text should therefore resemble nucleus depth-$(d{+}1)$ text in tail statistics.
    20	GPT-2~XL confirms this: temperature d1 median \texttt{p90\_ppl} (92.2) matches the nucleus d2 median (92.3, IQR 75.8--112.5).
    21	Top-$k$ truncation removes the low-probability tokens that carry depth signal, predicting weaker discriminability; top-$k$ cells yield the lowest accuracy (68.9--75.8\%) across the atlas (\S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).
```

    "newString": "construct a 180K cell-instance benchmark spanning",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nAs language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\\to$data$\\to$model loop arises, yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.\nWe formalize \\emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K cell-instances (140K unique passages) spanning three model families, three decoding strategies, and four depths under controlled full-replacement chains at the 1--1.5B parameter scale.\nA lightweight pipeline (19 statistical features classified by Random Forest) achieves d1--d3 balanced accuracy of 59--82\\% across nine model--strategy cells, with $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} = 3$ in 8/9 cells.\nThree findings emerge: (1)~depth signal concentrates in perplexity distribution tails, corroborated by classifier convergence and independent cross-perplexity features; (2)~decoding strategy affects cross-condition transfer more than model architecture, suggesting structurally distinct degradation trajectories; (3)~signal attenuates with scale (14B cross-reference accuracy ${\\sim}44$\\%, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$ for nucleus and temperature, 0 for top-$k$), with reference-model choice accounting for ${\\sim}7$\\,pp of the apparent degradation.\nOur depth estimation claims hold most strongly within-strategy or when the decoding configuration is known.\nPerturbation-based and membership-inference baselines lack ordinal depth resolution, confirming that depth estimation requires distributional statistics beyond binary detection scores.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

    "newString": "Our contribution centers on task formalization and systematic empirical characterization; the convergence of four classifiers within 0.8\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}) confirms that signal resides in feature design, not classifier architecture.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nThe text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.\nAs synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.\nThis model$\\to$data$\\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical; it is already occurring in the wild. Yet whether the recursive generation depth of a text can be estimated from its statistical properties remains unstudied, a question that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.\nIn this work, we focus on the 1--1.5B parameter regime under \\emph{controlled full-replacement chains}, where each generation fully replaces the prior training set, isolating the depth variable. Real-world contamination typically involves partial mixing (accumulation), where synthetic and human data co-exist at unknown proportions; the full-replacement setting provides an upper bound on depth signal strength, and we discuss implications for accumulation settings in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\nExisting tools address adjacent but distinct problems.\nBinary detectors \\cite{binoculars2024,fast_detectgpt2024,diveye2026} collapse all depths $d \\geq 1$ into a single category, a limitation we confirm empirically: their pairwise d1-vs-d2 AUC collapses to chance level (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}); watermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding.\nThe model collapse literature \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.\nNo existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone (Figure~\\ref{fig:problem}).\n\nWe find that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are \\textbf{consistent in direction but scale-dependent in magnitude} across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.\nSpecifically, the perplexity tail feature family (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance emerge as dominant depth indicators, with group-level dominance robust across all 9 model--strategy configurations and three importance measures.\nFollowing the tradition of benchmark-driven empirical studies in NLP, our contribution centers on task formalization and systematic empirical characterization rather than algorithmic novelty; the convergence of four classifiers within 0.8\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}) confirms that discriminative signal resides in feature design, not classifier architecture.\nWe formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and find that a lightweight pipeline suffices: compute 19 reference-model statistical features (effective dimensionality ${\\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.\nTo characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families $\\times$ decoding strategies spanning 4 depths (180K cell-instances).\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories and we introduce \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD), to our knowledge the first operational metric for ordinal depth resolution in recursive generation, to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.68\\textwidth]{figures/fig_problem_illustration.pdf}\n\\caption{Recursive generation depth estimation. A seed corpus $\\mathcal{D}_0$ feeds a LoRA finetuning--sampling loop producing successively deeper corpora. Given a passage~$x$, the task is to estimate its ordinal depth~$d$.}\n\\label{fig:problem}\n\\end{figure*}\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization and metric.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, and construct a 180K cell-instance benchmark ($3$ model families $\\times$ $3$ decoding strategies $\\times$ $4$ depths; 140K unique passages).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} The perplexity tail feature family and surprisal variance jointly dominate depth discrimination across all 9 cells; $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ in 8/9 cells (4 under nucleus depth extension), validated by 5-class extension (71--80\\%); feature group ablation confirms perplexity tail dominance (76.6\\% alone vs.\\ 78.4\\% full set).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Systematic characterization.} Cross-model transfer 66.4\\% (d1--d3: 55.4\\%), cross-domain arXiv 74.1\\%, 14B cross-reference 44.1\\% ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$); 25--50\\% accumulation preserves $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ in 5/6 cells despite $-$8--15\\,pp accuracy attenuation. We identify cross-strategy transfer (d1--d3: 45.9\\%) as a boundary condition.\n\\end{itemize}",

    "oldString": "All AUC profiles in our primary benchmark (9 cells $\\times$ depths 0--3 plus 3 nucleus cells $\\times$ depths 0--5 $=$ 12 profiles) decay monotonically; non-monotonic profiles (U-shaped recovery in Qwen2.5-7B, non-monotonic decay in Llama-3.1-8B base; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) occur only in cross-scale extensions outside the primary atlas.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nWe use \\emph{depth estimation} as shorthand throughout; the underlying task is ordinal classification.\nDepths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\nOur findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K cell-instances; 140K unique passages, as d0 samples are drawn from a shared Wikipedia seed corpus across all cells).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend all three models under nucleus sampling to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each cycle: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, effective batch 16, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$; fresh adapter from the base model at each depth; full protocol in Appendix~\\ref{app:lora_protocol}); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages (max 512 tokens, beginning-of-sequence (BOS) seeded) under the specified strategy; (3)~$\\mathcal{D}_k$ feeds the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model trained on prior-depth data, not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nBeginning-of-sequence seeding may amplify depth signal relative to prompted generation; prompted-generation validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompted_gen}) confirms signal persistence albeit attenuated ($-$13.8\\,pp).\nNo per-passage lineage exists across depths; cross-validation splits do not introduce leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) use Pythia-1.4B as fixed reference model.\nThis overlaps with one generator (Pythia), conferring a 4--11\\,pp self-reference advantage on that pipeline (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}); Pythia results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} are marked $^\\dagger$ accordingly.\nAlternative references yield 67--79\\%, all exceeding $2.5{\\times}$ chance (Limitations).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\\% of full-set performance, and variance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features; effective independent dimensionality is ${\\approx}$4--5.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (Pythia-1.4B).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular*}{\\columnwidth}{@{\\extracolsep{\\fill}}llr}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular*}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles are especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe propose the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) as an operational measure of how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\nEach pair uses an independent classifier to avoid decision boundary dilution from multi-class settings.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{multline}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\\\\n\\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{multline}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$. If\\, $\\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.\n\\end{definition}\n\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ reports the longest \\emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix: the deepest $d$ such that every pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ exceeds $T$.\nAll AUC profiles in our primary benchmark (9 cells $\\times$ depths 0--3 plus 3 nucleus cells $\\times$ depths 0--5 $=$ 12 profiles) decay monotonically; non-monotonic profiles (U-shaped recovery in Qwen2.5-7B, non-monotonic decay in Llama-3.1-8B base; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) occur only in cross-scale extensions outside the primary atlas.\nWe adopt $T{=}0.70$ following the ``acceptable discrimination'' threshold in ROC analysis \\cite{hosmer2000} (8/9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} \\geq 3$); sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) shows $\\mathrm{EDD}$ shifts by at most one level across $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.80\\}$ in all nine cells.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold CV; EDD binary classifiers use 500 trees).\\footnote{Statistical tests are not corrected for multiple comparisons; $p$-values should be interpreted as exploratory.}\nThe RF produces ordinally consistent predictions: 98.2\\% of errors fall on adjacent depths ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$), quadratic weighted kappa (QWK)\\,=\\,0.89.\nComparisons against other classifiers and detection baselines appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nAs language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\\to$data$\\to$model loop arises, yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.\nWe formalize \\emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a 180K cell-instance benchmark spanning three model families, three decoding strategies, and four depths under controlled full-replacement chains at the 1--1.5B parameter scale.\nA lightweight pipeline (19 statistical features classified by Random Forest) achieves d1--d3 balanced accuracy of 59--82\\% across nine model--strategy cells, with $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} = 3$ in 8/9 cells.\nThree findings emerge: (1)~depth signal concentrates in perplexity distribution tails, corroborated by classifier convergence and independent cross-perplexity features; (2)~decoding strategy affects cross-condition transfer more than model architecture, suggesting structurally distinct degradation trajectories; (3)~signal attenuates with scale (14B cross-reference accuracy ${\\sim}44$\\%, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$ for nucleus and temperature, 0 for top-$k$), with reference-model choice accounting for ${\\sim}7$\\,pp of the apparent degradation.\nOur depth estimation claims hold most strongly within-strategy or when the decoding configuration is known.\nPerturbation-based and membership-inference baselines lack ordinal depth resolution, confirming that depth estimation requires distributional statistics beyond binary detection scores.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nThe text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.\nAs synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.\nThis model$\\to$data$\\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical; it is already occurring in the wild. Yet whether the recursive generation depth of a text can be estimated from its statistical properties remains unstudied, a question that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.\nIn this work, we focus on the 1--1.5B parameter regime under \\emph{controlled full-replacement chains}, where each generation fully replaces the prior training set, isolating the depth variable. Real-world contamination typically involves partial mixing (accumulation), where synthetic and human data co-exist at unknown proportions; the full-replacement setting provides an upper bound on depth signal strength, and we discuss implications for accumulation settings in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\nExisting tools address adjacent but distinct problems.\nBinary detectors \\cite{binoculars2024,fast_detectgpt2024,diveye2026} collapse all depths $d \\geq 1$ into a single category, a limitation we confirm empirically: their pairwise d1-vs-d2 AUC collapses to chance level (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}); watermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding.\nThe model collapse literature \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.\nNo existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone (Figure~\\ref{fig:problem}).\n\nWe find that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are \\textbf{consistent in direction but scale-dependent in magnitude} across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.\nSpecifically, the perplexity tail feature family (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance emerge as dominant depth indicators, with group-level dominance robust across all 9 model--strategy configurations and three importance measures.\nOur contribution centers on task formalization and systematic empirical characterization; the convergence of four classifiers within 0.8\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}) confirms that signal resides in feature design, not classifier architecture.\nWe formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and find that a lightweight pipeline suffices: compute 19 reference-model statistical features (effective dimensionality ${\\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.\nTo characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families $\\times$ decoding strategies spanning 4 depths (180K cell-instances).\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories and we introduce \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD), to our knowledge the first operational metric for ordinal depth resolution in recursive generation, to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.68\\textwidth]{figures/fig_problem_illustration.pdf}\n\\caption{Recursive generation depth estimation. A seed corpus $\\mathcal{D}_0$ feeds a LoRA finetuning--sampling loop producing successively deeper corpora. Given a passage~$x$, the task is to estimate its ordinal depth~$d$.}\n\\label{fig:problem}\n\\end{figure*}\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization and metric.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, and construct a 180K cell-instance benchmark ($3$ model families $\\times$ $3$ decoding strategies $\\times$ $4$ depths).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} The perplexity tail feature family and surprisal variance jointly dominate depth discrimination across all 9 cells; $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ in 8/9 cells (4 under nucleus depth extension), validated by 5-class extension (71--80\\%); feature group ablation confirms perplexity tail dominance (76.6\\% alone vs.\\ 78.4\\% full set).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Systematic characterization.} Cross-model transfer 66.4\\% (d1--d3: 55.4\\%), cross-domain arXiv 74.1\\%, 14B cross-reference 44.1\\% ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$); 25--50\\% accumulation preserves $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ in 5/6 cells despite $-$8--15\\,pp accuracy attenuation. We identify cross-strategy transfer (d1--d3: 45.9\\%) as a boundary condition.\n\\end{itemize}",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nWe use \\emph{depth estimation} as shorthand throughout; the underlying task is ordinal classification.\nDepths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\nOur findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K cell-instances; 140K unique passages, as d0 samples are drawn from a shared Wikipedia seed corpus across all cells).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend all three models under nucleus sampling to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each cycle: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, effective batch 16, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$; fresh adapter from the base model at each depth; full protocol in Appendix~\\ref{app:lora_protocol}); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages (max 512 tokens, beginning-of-sequence (BOS) seeded) under the specified strategy; (3)~$\\mathcal{D}_k$ feeds the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model trained on prior-depth data, not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nBeginning-of-sequence seeding may amplify depth signal relative to prompted generation; prompted-generation validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompted_gen}) confirms signal persistence albeit attenuated ($-$13.8\\,pp).\nNo per-passage lineage exists across depths; cross-validation splits do not introduce leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) use Pythia-1.4B as fixed reference model.\nThis overlaps with one generator (Pythia), conferring a 4--11\\,pp self-reference advantage on that pipeline (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}); Pythia results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} are marked $^\\dagger$ accordingly.\nAlternative references yield 67--79\\%, all exceeding $2.5{\\times}$ chance (Limitations).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\\% of full-set performance, and variance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features; effective independent dimensionality is ${\\approx}$4--5.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (Pythia-1.4B).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular*}{\\columnwidth}{@{\\extracolsep{\\fill}}llr}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular*}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles are especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe propose the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) as an operational measure of how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\nEach pair uses an independent classifier to avoid decision boundary dilution from multi-class settings.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{multline}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\\\\n\\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{multline}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$. If\\, $\\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.\n\\end{definition}\n\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ reports the longest \\emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix: the deepest $d$ such that every pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ exceeds $T$.\nAll AUC profiles in the primary benchmark decay monotonically; non-monotonic profiles occur only in cross-scale extensions (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).\nWe adopt $T{=}0.70$ following the ``acceptable discrimination'' threshold in ROC analysis \\cite{hosmer2000} (8/9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} \\geq 3$); sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) shows $\\mathrm{EDD}$ shifts by at most one level across $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.80\\}$ in all nine cells.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold CV; EDD binary classifiers use 500 trees).\\footnote{Statistical tests are not corrected for multiple comparisons; $p$-values should be interpreted as exploratory.}\nThe RF produces ordinally consistent predictions: 98.2\\% of errors fall on adjacent depths ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$), quadratic weighted kappa (QWK)\\,=\\,0.89.\nComparisons against other classifiers and detection baselines appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Multi-channel depth encoding.}
5	Three importance measures (Gini, SHAP, permutation; Kendall's $W{=}0.90$), confirmed by a fourth (CPI; Appendix~\ref{app:conditional_pi}), converge on the same feature ranking, and an independent Binoculars-derived 11-feature pathway reaches statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1\% vs.\ 78.4\%, $p{>}0.05$; merged: 82.6\%).
6	Recursive generation thus leaves fingerprints across multiple statistical channels; the depth signal is a distributional property, not a single exploitable artifact.
7	
8	\paragraph{Deployment considerations.}
9	The strategy effect (9.3\,pp) exceeding the model effect (7.2\,pp) means auditing pipelines must condition on decoding configuration, motivating the two-stage pipeline (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) and echoing the call by \citet{schaeffer2025} for operationally precise metrics.
10	A preliminary curation PoC (Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}) is inconclusive: RF-filtered subsets outperform random subsampling but do not significantly improve over unfiltered data ($p{>}0.4$).
11	Scale attenuation at 14B (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), consistent with tail-narrowing theory \citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, may be partially recoverable via scale-matched references.
12	%% --- 7B SCALE TEMPLATES (swap in when results arrive) ---
13	%% VERSION A (7B ≥60%, EDD≥2): replace the sentence above with:
14	%% Scale attenuation is monotonic across the three scales tested: self-reference accuracy declines from 78.7\% (1.4B) through $X$\% (7B, $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}Y$) to 50.8\% (14B, $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$), consistent with tail-narrowing theory \citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; the within-family Qwen 7B$\to$14B comparison ($-Z$\,pp) isolates a scale effect free of architecture confounds, and whether scale-matched references can recover further signal remains open.
15	%% VERSION B (7B 40-60%): replace with:
16	%% Scale attenuation is confirmed at an intermediate scale: 7B self-reference accuracy reaches $X$\% ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}Y$), between 1.4B (78.7\%) and 14B (50.8\%), consistent with monotonic attenuation \citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} though insufficient to characterize its functional form; whether scale-matched references can recover further signal remains open.
17	%% --- END TEMPLATES ---
18	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nWe use \\emph{depth estimation} as shorthand throughout; the underlying task is ordinal classification.\nDepths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\nOur findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K cell-instances; 140K unique passages, as d0 samples are drawn from a shared Wikipedia seed corpus across all cells).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend all three models under nucleus sampling to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each cycle: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, effective batch 16, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$; fresh adapter from the base model at each depth; full protocol in Appendix~\\ref{app:lora_protocol}); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages (max 512 tokens, beginning-of-sequence (BOS) seeded) under the specified strategy; (3)~$\\mathcal{D}_k$ feeds the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model trained on prior-depth data, not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nBeginning-of-sequence seeding may amplify depth signal relative to prompted generation; prompted-generation validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompted_gen}) confirms signal persistence albeit attenuated ($-$13.8\\,pp).\nNo per-passage lineage exists across depths; cross-validation splits do not introduce leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) use Pythia-1.4B as fixed reference model.\nThis overlaps with one generator (Pythia), conferring a 4--11\\,pp self-reference advantage on that pipeline (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}); Pythia results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} are marked $^\\dagger$ accordingly.\nAlternative references yield 67--79\\%, all exceeding $2.5{\\times}$ chance (Limitations).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\\% of full-set performance, and variance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features; effective independent dimensionality is ${\\approx}$4--5.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (Pythia-1.4B).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular*}{\\columnwidth}{@{\\extracolsep{\\fill}}llr}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular*}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles are especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe propose the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) as an operational measure of how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\nEach pair uses an independent classifier to avoid decision boundary dilution from multi-class settings.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{multline}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\\\\n\\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{multline}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$. If\\, $\\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.\n\\end{definition}\n\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ reports the longest \\emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix: the deepest $d$ such that every pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ exceeds $T$.\nAll AUC profiles in our primary benchmark (9 cells $\\times$ depths 0--3 plus 3 nucleus cells $\\times$ depths 0--5) decay monotonically; non-monotonic profiles occur only in cross-scale extensions outside the primary atlas (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).\nWe adopt $T{=}0.70$ following the ``acceptable discrimination'' threshold in ROC analysis \\cite{hosmer2000} (8/9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} \\geq 3$); sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) shows $\\mathrm{EDD}$ shifts by at most one level across $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.80\\}$ in all nine cells.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold CV; EDD binary classifiers use 500 trees).\\footnote{Statistical tests are not corrected for multiple comparisons; $p$-values should be interpreted as exploratory.}\nThe RF produces ordinally consistent predictions: 98.2\\% of errors fall on adjacent depths ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$), quadratic weighted kappa (QWK)\\,=\\,0.89.\nComparisons against other classifiers and detection baselines appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",
