    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nThe text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.\nAs synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.\nThis model$\\to$data$\\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical; it is already occurring in the wild. Yet whether the recursive generation depth of a text can be estimated from its statistical properties remains unstudied, a question that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.\nIn this work, we focus on the 1--1.5B parameter regime under \\emph{controlled full-replacement chains}, where each generation fully replaces the prior training set, isolating the depth variable. Real-world contamination typically involves partial mixing (accumulation), where synthetic and human data co-exist at unknown proportions; the full-replacement setting provides an upper bound on depth signal strength, and we discuss implications for accumulation settings in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\nExisting tools address adjacent but distinct problems.\nBinary detectors \\cite{binoculars2024,fast_detectgpt2024,diveye2026} collapse all depths $d \\geq 1$ into a single category, a limitation we confirm empirically: their pairwise d1-vs-d2 AUC collapses to chance level (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}); watermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding.\nThe model collapse literature \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.\nNo existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone (Figure~\\ref{fig:problem}).\n\nWe find that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are \\textbf{consistent in direction but scale-dependent in magnitude} across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.\nSpecifically, the perplexity tail feature family (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance emerge as dominant depth indicators, with group-level dominance robust across all 9 model--strategy configurations and three importance measures.\nOur contribution centers on task formalization and systematic empirical characterization; the convergence of four classifiers within 0.8\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}) confirms that signal resides in feature design, not classifier architecture.\nWe formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and find that a lightweight pipeline suffices: compute 19 reference-model statistical features (effective dimensionality ${\\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.\nTo characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families $\\times$ decoding strategies spanning 4 depths (180K cell-instances).\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories and we introduce \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD), to our knowledge the first operational metric for ordinal depth resolution in recursive generation, to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.68\\textwidth]{figures/fig_problem_illustration.pdf}\n\\caption{Recursive generation depth estimation. A seed corpus $\\mathcal{D}_0$ feeds a LoRA finetuning--sampling loop producing successively deeper corpora. Given a passage~$x$, the task is to estimate its ordinal depth~$d$.}\n\\label{fig:problem}\n\\end{figure*}\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization and metric.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, and construct a 180K cell-instance benchmark ($3$ model families $\\times$ $3$ decoding strategies $\\times$ $4$ depths; 140K unique passages).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} The perplexity tail feature family and surprisal variance jointly dominate depth discrimination across all 9 cells; $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ in 8/9 cells (4 under nucleus depth extension), validated by 5-class extension (71--80\\%); feature group ablation confirms perplexity tail dominance (76.6\\% alone vs.\\ 78.4\\% full set).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Systematic characterization.} Cross-model transfer 66.4\\% (d1--d3: 55.4\\%), cross-domain arXiv 74.1\\%, 14B cross-reference 44.1\\% ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$); 25--50\\% accumulation preserves $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ in 5/6 cells despite $-$8--15\\,pp accuracy attenuation. We identify cross-strategy transfer (d1--d3: 45.9\\%) as a boundary condition.\n\\end{itemize}",

    "newString": "The atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories; we introduce \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nThe text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.\nAs synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.\nThis model$\\to$data$\\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical; it is already occurring in the wild. Yet whether the recursive generation depth of a text can be estimated from its statistical properties remains unstudied, a question that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.\nWe focus on the 1--1.5B parameter regime under \\emph{controlled full-replacement chains}, where each generation fully replaces the prior training set, isolating the depth variable. Real-world contamination involves partial mixing at unknown proportions; full replacement provides an upper bound on depth signal strength (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nExisting tools address adjacent but distinct problems.\nBinary detectors \\cite{binoculars2024,fast_detectgpt2024,diveye2026} collapse all depths $d \\geq 1$ into a single category, a limitation we confirm empirically: their pairwise d1-vs-d2 AUC collapses to chance level (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}); watermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding.\nThe model collapse literature \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.\nNo existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone (Figure~\\ref{fig:problem}).\n\nWe find that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are \\textbf{consistent in direction but scale-dependent in magnitude} across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.\nSpecifically, the perplexity tail feature family (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance emerge as dominant depth indicators, with group-level dominance robust across all 9 model--strategy configurations and three importance measures.\nOur contribution centers on task formalization and systematic empirical characterization; the convergence of four classifiers within 0.8\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}) confirms that signal resides in feature design, not classifier architecture.\nWe formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and find that a lightweight pipeline suffices: compute 19 reference-model statistical features (effective dimensionality ${\\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.\nTo characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families $\\times$ decoding strategies spanning 4 depths (180K cell-instances).\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories and we introduce \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD), to our knowledge the first operational metric for ordinal depth resolution in recursive generation, to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.68\\textwidth]{figures/fig_problem_illustration.pdf}\n\\caption{Recursive generation depth estimation. A seed corpus $\\mathcal{D}_0$ feeds a LoRA finetuning--sampling loop producing successively deeper corpora. Given a passage~$x$, the task is to estimate its ordinal depth~$d$.}\n\\label{fig:problem}\n\\end{figure*}\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization and metric.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, and construct a 180K cell-instance benchmark ($3$ model families $\\times$ $3$ decoding strategies $\\times$ $4$ depths; 140K unique passages).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} The perplexity tail feature family and surprisal variance jointly dominate depth discrimination across all 9 cells; $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ in 8/9 cells (4 under nucleus depth extension), validated by 5-class extension (71--80\\%); feature group ablation confirms perplexity tail dominance (76.6\\% alone vs.\\ 78.4\\% full set).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Systematic characterization.} Cross-model transfer 66.4\\% (d1--d3: 55.4\\%), cross-domain arXiv 74.1\\%, 14B cross-reference 44.1\\% ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$); 25--50\\% accumulation preserves $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ in 5/6 cells despite $-$8--15\\,pp accuracy attenuation. We identify cross-strategy transfer (d1--d3: 45.9\\%) as a boundary condition.\n\\end{itemize}",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_015N5xW7cjutRRhzFEkYgWFG`</summary>

```text
1	\subsection{Theoretical Motivation}
2	\label{sec:method:theory}
3	
4	Recursive generation contracts the support of the learned distribution, with low-probability tokens lost first \cite{shumailov2024_nature}: tail mass $P_k(\mathrm{ppl} > t)$ decreases monotonically in depth $k$.
5	This tail erosion predicts the perplexity tail feature family as a depth marker, confirmed jointly with surprisal variance across all 9 cells (\S\ref{sec:atlas:features}).
6	Surprisal variance captures complementary distributional narrowing: as the support contracts, per-token surprise becomes more predictable.
7	
8	\paragraph{EDD upper bound.}
9	Tail mass at depth $k$ scales as $\alpha^k$ for a contraction factor $\alpha \in (0,1)$ \cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; EDD reaches its upper bound when adjacent-depth feature distance falls below classifier resolution.
10	Our empirical AUC-excess contraction (ratio 0.80 at d1v2$\to$d2v3, 0.69 at d3v4$\to$d4v5; \S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) is consistent with geometric decay approaching a fixed point \cite{bertrand2024_stability}.
11	5-class extensions to d4 (71--80\%, chance = 20\%; Appendix~\ref{app:depth4_ext}) confirm depth~4 discriminability; mean perplexity saturates before tail statistics, placing a physical ceiling on EDD.
12	
13	\paragraph{Scale-dependent attenuation.}
14	Larger models approximate the training distribution more faithfully per step ($\alpha$ closer to 1), predicting weaker per-step signal.
15	Qwen2.5-14B supports this: d0-vs-d1 AUC drops to 0.643 (vs.\ ${>}0.99$ at 1--1.5B; \S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}); however, 7--8B results are mixed ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ ranging from 4 to 5), suggesting confounds (QLoRA quantization, architecture) interact with the scale effect (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).
16	
17	\paragraph{Strategy-dependent convergence.}
18	The contraction rate $\alpha$ varies with decoding strategy.
19	Temperature scaling ($\tau < 1$) sharpens the logit distribution uniformly and accelerates convergence to the mode; temperature depth-$d$ text should therefore resemble nucleus depth-$(d{+}1)$ text in tail statistics.
20	GPT-2~XL confirms this: temperature d1 median \texttt{p90\_ppl} (92.2) matches the nucleus d2 median (92.3, IQR 75.8--112.5).
21	Top-$k$ truncation removes the low-probability tokens that carry depth signal, predicting weaker discriminability; top-$k$ cells yield the lowest accuracy (68.9--75.8\%) across the atlas (\S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).
22	
```

    "originalFile": "\\subsection{Theoretical Motivation}\n\\label{sec:method:theory}\n\nRecursive generation contracts the support of the learned distribution, with low-probability tokens lost first \\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: tail mass $P_k(\\mathrm{ppl} > t)$ decreases monotonically in depth $k$.\nThis tail erosion predicts the perplexity tail feature family as a depth marker, confirmed jointly with surprisal variance across all 9 cells (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}).\nSurprisal variance captures complementary distributional narrowing: as the support contracts, per-token surprise becomes more predictable.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD upper bound.}\nTail mass at depth $k$ scales as $\\alpha^k$ for a contraction factor $\\alpha \\in (0,1)$ \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; EDD reaches its upper bound when adjacent-depth feature distance falls below classifier resolution.\nOur empirical AUC-excess contraction (ratio 0.80 at d1v2$\\to$d2v3, 0.69 at d3v4$\\to$d4v5; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) is consistent with geometric decay approaching a fixed point \\cite{bertrand2024_stability}.\n5-class extensions to d4 (71--80\\%, chance = 20\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:depth4_ext}) confirm depth~4 discriminability; mean perplexity saturates before tail statistics, placing a physical ceiling on EDD.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale-dependent attenuation.}\nLarger models approximate the training distribution more faithfully per step ($\\alpha$ closer to 1), predicting weaker per-step signal.\nQwen2.5-14B supports this: d0-vs-d1 AUC drops to 0.643 (vs.\\ ${>}0.99$ at 1--1.5B; \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}); 7--8B results are mixed, suggesting additional confounds interact with the scale effect (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-dependent convergence.}\nThe contraction rate $\\alpha$ varies with decoding strategy.\nTemperature scaling ($\\tau < 1$) sharpens the logit distribution uniformly and accelerates convergence to the mode; temperature depth-$d$ text should therefore resemble nucleus depth-$(d{+}1)$ text in tail statistics.\nGPT-2~XL confirms this: temperature d1 median \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (92.2) matches the nucleus d2 median (92.3, IQR 75.8--112.5).\nTop-$k$ truncation removes the low-probability tokens that carry depth signal, predicting weaker discriminability; top-$k$ cells yield the lowest accuracy (68.9--75.8\\%) across the atlas (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\n",

{

    "oldString": "Beginning-of-sequence seeding may amplify depth signal relative to prompted generation; prompted-generation validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompted_gen}) confirms signal persistence albeit attenuated ($-$13.8\\,pp).",
    "newString": "BOS seeding may amplify depth signal; prompted-generation validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompted_gen}) confirms signal persistence albeit attenuated ($-$13.8\\,pp).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nWe use \\emph{depth estimation} as shorthand throughout; the underlying task is ordinal classification.\nDepths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\nOur findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K cell-instances; 140K unique passages, as d0 samples are drawn from a shared Wikipedia seed corpus across all cells).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend all three models under nucleus sampling to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each cycle: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, effective batch 16, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$; fresh adapter from the base model at each depth; full protocol in Appendix~\\ref{app:lora_protocol}); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages (max 512 tokens, beginning-of-sequence (BOS) seeded) under the specified strategy; (3)~$\\mathcal{D}_k$ feeds the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model trained on prior-depth data, not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nBeginning-of-sequence seeding may amplify depth signal relative to prompted generation; prompted-generation validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompted_gen}) confirms signal persistence albeit attenuated ($-$13.8\\,pp).\nNo per-passage lineage exists across depths; cross-validation splits do not introduce leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) use Pythia-1.4B as fixed reference model.\nThis overlaps with one generator (Pythia), conferring a 4--11\\,pp self-reference advantage on that pipeline (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}); Pythia results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} are marked $^\\dagger$ accordingly.\nAlternative references yield 67--79\\%, all exceeding $2.5{\\times}$ chance (Limitations).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\\% of full-set performance, and variance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features; effective independent dimensionality is ${\\approx}$4--5.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (Pythia-1.4B).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular*}{\\columnwidth}{@{\\extracolsep{\\fill}}llr}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular*}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles are especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe propose the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) as an operational measure of how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\nEach pair uses an independent classifier to avoid decision boundary dilution from multi-class settings.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{multline}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\\\\n\\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{multline}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$. If\\, $\\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.\n\\end{definition}\n\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ reports the longest \\emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix: the deepest $d$ such that every pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ exceeds $T$.\nAll AUC profiles in the primary benchmark decay monotonically; non-monotonic profiles occur only in cross-scale extensions (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).\nWe adopt $T{=}0.70$ following the ``acceptable discrimination'' threshold in ROC analysis \\cite{hosmer2000} (8/9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70} \\geq 3$); sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) shows $\\mathrm{EDD}$ shifts by at most one level across $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.80\\}$ in all nine cells.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold CV; EDD binary classifiers use 500 trees).\\footnote{Statistical tests are not corrected for multiple comparisons; $p$-values should be interpreted as exploratory.}\nThe RF produces ordinally consistent predictions: 98.2\\% of errors fall on adjacent depths ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$), quadratic weighted kappa (QWK)\\,=\\,0.89.\nComparisons against other classifiers and detection baselines appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FRod7AFLLQM86FV4tr79jn`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Multi-channel depth encoding.}
5	Three importance measures (Gini, SHAP, permutation; Kendall's $W{=}0.90$), confirmed by a fourth (CPI; Appendix~\ref{app:conditional_pi}), converge on the same feature ranking, and an independent Binoculars-derived 11-feature pathway reaches statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1\% vs.\ 78.4\%, $p{>}0.05$; merged: 82.6\%).
6	Recursive generation thus leaves fingerprints across multiple statistical channels; the depth signal is a distributional property, not a single exploitable artifact.
7	
8	\paragraph{Deployment considerations.}
9	The strategy effect (9.3\,pp) exceeding the model effect (7.2\,pp) means auditing pipelines must condition on decoding configuration, motivating the two-stage pipeline (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) and echoing the call by \citet{schaeffer2025} for operationally precise metrics.
10	A preliminary curation PoC (Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}) is inconclusive: RF-filtered subsets outperform random subsampling but do not significantly improve over unfiltered data ($p{>}0.4$).
11	Scale attenuation at 14B (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), consistent with tail-narrowing theory \citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, may be partially recoverable via scale-matched references.
12	
13	\paragraph{Outlook.}
14	Depth estimation bridges binary synthetic text detection and the model collapse literature by providing ordinal resolution.
15	Rather than discarding all machine-generated text, practitioners can selectively filter deeply recursive content while retaining shallow-depth text that may preserve training utility \cite{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse}.
16	Extending the framework to accumulation settings, prompted generation, and frontier-scale models remains the principal open direction.
17	%% --- 7B SCALE TEMPLATES (swap in when results arrive) ---
18	%% VERSION A (7B ≥60%, EDD≥2): replace the sentence above with:
19	%% Scale attenuation is monotonic across the three scales tested: self-reference accuracy declines from 78.7\% (1.4B) through $X$\% (7B, $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}Y$) to 50.8\% (14B, $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$), consistent with tail-narrowing theory \citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; the within-family Qwen 7B$\to$14B comparison ($-Z$\,pp) isolates a scale effect free of architecture confounds, and whether scale-matched references can recover further signal remains open.
20	%% VERSION B (7B 40-60%): replace with:
21	%% Scale attenuation is confirmed at an intermediate scale: 7B self-reference accuracy reaches $X$\% ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}Y$), between 1.4B (78.7\%) and 14B (50.8\%), consistent with monotonic attenuation \citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} though insufficient to characterize its functional form; whether scale-matched references can recover further signal remains open.
22	%% --- END TEMPLATES ---
23	
```

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Outlook.}\nDepth estimation bridges binary synthetic text detection and the model collapse literature by providing ordinal resolution.\nRather than discarding all machine-generated text, practitioners can selectively filter deeply recursive content while retaining shallow-depth text that may preserve training utility \\cite{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse}.\nExtending the framework to accumulation settings, prompted generation, and frontier-scale models remains the principal open direction.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Outlook.}\nDepth estimation bridges binary synthetic text detection and the model collapse literature by providing ordinal resolution, enabling selective filtering of deeply recursive content while retaining shallow-depth text \\cite{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse}.\nExtending to accumulation settings, prompted generation, and frontier-scale models remains the principal open direction.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-channel depth encoding.}\nThree importance measures (Gini, SHAP, permutation; Kendall's $W{=}0.90$), confirmed by a fourth (CPI; Appendix~\\ref{app:conditional_pi}), converge on the same feature ranking, and an independent Binoculars-derived 11-feature pathway reaches statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1\\% vs.\\ 78.4\\%, $p{>}0.05$; merged: 82.6\\%).\nRecursive generation thus leaves fingerprints across multiple statistical channels; the depth signal is a distributional property, not a single exploitable artifact.\n\n\\paragraph{Deployment considerations.}\nThe strategy effect (9.3\\,pp) exceeding the model effect (7.2\\,pp) means auditing pipelines must condition on decoding configuration, motivating the two-stage pipeline (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) and echoing the call by \\citet{schaeffer2025} for operationally precise metrics.\nA preliminary curation PoC (Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}) is inconclusive: RF-filtered subsets outperform random subsampling but do not significantly improve over unfiltered data ($p{>}0.4$).\nScale attenuation at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), consistent with tail-narrowing theory \\citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, may be partially recoverable via scale-matched references.\n\n\\paragraph{Outlook.}\nDepth estimation bridges binary synthetic text detection and the model collapse literature by providing ordinal resolution.\nRather than discarding all machine-generated text, practitioners can selectively filter deeply recursive content while retaining shallow-depth text that may preserve training utility \\cite{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse}.\nExtending the framework to accumulation settings, prompted generation, and frontier-scale models remains the principal open direction.\n%% --- 7B SCALE TEMPLATES (swap in when results arrive) ---\n%% VERSION A (7B ≥60%, EDD≥2): replace the sentence above with:\n%% Scale attenuation is monotonic across the three scales tested: self-reference accuracy declines from 78.7\\% (1.4B) through $X$\\% (7B, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}Y$) to 50.8\\% (14B, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$), consistent with tail-narrowing theory \\citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; _source line 8130 · 2026-05-22T17:58:40.615Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019CXJT4mU8VrHucMQ8a4ybk`</summary>

```text
     1	\section*{Limitations}
     2	\label{sec:limitations}
     3	
     4	\paragraph{Full-replacement vs.\ accumulation.}
     5	The primary benchmark uses full-replacement chains; real-world contamination involves accumulation at unknown mixing ratios \cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate}.
     6	At 25--50\% synthetic mixing, accuracy drops 8--15\,pp and $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ is preserved in 5/6 tested cells (GPT-2~XL nucleus at 50\% drops to $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$; d2v3 AUC\,=\,0.695, marginally below threshold; Table~\ref{tab:accum_auc}); accumulation experiments use matched strategies per model (nucleus for GPT-2~XL/Pythia, temperature for OLMo) and do not test cross-strategy accumulation; extension to larger models and higher mixing ratios remains future work.
     7	
     8	\paragraph{Scale, generalization, and reference model.}
     9	At 14B, cross-reference accuracy drops to 44.1\% ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$), with reference-model mismatch accounting for ${\sim}$7\,pp; cross-strategy transfer (d1--d3: 45.9\%) can invert d1/d2 predictions (d1v2 AUC as low as 0.13 for temperature text, 0.25 for OLMo, classified by a nucleus-trained model), requiring strategy-aware deployment.
    10	The pipeline requires a fixed reference model; cross-architecture references degrade accuracy by 4--11\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:ref_ablation}).
    11	Additional caveats (single LoRA seed with ${\sim}$1--3\,pp uncertainty, web-crawl false positive rates, passage length sensitivity) are detailed in the Appendix.
    12	
    13	\section*{Ethics Statement}
    14	
    15	This work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.
    16	No human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.
    17	Our method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.
    18	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
    19	AI assistants were used in an auxiliary role for coding support and language polishing.
    20	All scientific decisions, experimental design, analysis, and core writing were conducted by the authors.
```

</details>

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Full-replacement vs.\\ accumulation.}\nThe primary benchmark uses full-replacement chains; real-world contamination involves accumulation at unknown mixing ratios \\cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate}.\nAt 25--50\\% synthetic mixing, accuracy drops 8--15\\,pp and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ is preserved in 5/6 tested cells (GPT-2~XL nucleus at 50\\% drops to $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$; d2v3 AUC\\,=\\,0.695, marginally below threshold; Table~\\ref{tab:accum_auc}); accumulation experiments use matched strategies per model (nucleus for GPT-2~XL/Pythia, temperature for OLMo) and do not test cross-strategy accumulation; extension to larger models and higher mixing ratios remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale, generalization, and reference model.}\nAt 14B, cross-reference accuracy drops to 44.1\\% ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$), with reference-model mismatch accounting for ${\\sim}$7\\,pp; cross-strategy transfer (d1--d3: 45.9\\%) can invert d1/d2 predictions (d1v2 AUC as low as 0.13 for temperature text, 0.25 for OLMo, classified by a nucleus-trained model), requiring strategy-aware deployment.\nThe pipeline requires a fixed reference model; cross-architecture references degrade accuracy by 4--11\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}).\nAdditional caveats (single LoRA seed with ${\\sim}$1--3\\,pp uncertainty, web-crawl false positive rates, passage length sensitivity) are detailed in the Appendix.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Full-replacement vs.\\ accumulation.}\nThe primary benchmark uses full-replacement chains; real-world contamination involves accumulation at unknown mixing ratios \\cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate}.\nAccumulation experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:accumulation}) show accuracy attenuation that increases with higher synthetic mixing ratios, and do not test cross-strategy accumulation; extension to larger models and higher mixing ratios remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale, generalization, and reference model.}\nDepth signal attenuates at larger scales (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), with reference-model mismatch contributing to the observed degradation.\nCross-strategy transfer can invert adjacent-depth predictions when the classifier's training strategy differs from the test data, requiring strategy-aware deployment.\nThe pipeline requires a fixed reference model; cross-architecture references degrade accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}).\nAdditional caveats (single LoRA seed, web-crawl false positive rates, passage length sensitivity) are detailed in the Appendix."
}

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Full-replacement vs.\\ accumulation.}\nThe primary benchmark uses full-replacement chains; real-world contamination involves accumulation at unknown mixing ratios \\cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate}.\nAt 25--50\\% synthetic mixing, accuracy drops 8--15\\,pp and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}3$ is preserved in 5/6 tested cells (GPT-2~XL nucleus at 50\\% drops to $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$;
